Norm inflation for the cubic nonlinear heat equation above the scaling critical regularity
Ilya Chevyrev, Tadahiro Oh, and Yuzhao Wang

Abstract
. We consider the ill-posedness issue for the cubic nonlinear heat equation and prove norm inflation with infinite loss of regularity in the Hölder-Besov space 𝒞 s = B ∞ , ∞ s \mathcal{C}^{s}=B^{s}_{\infty,\infty} for s ≤ − 2 3 s\leq-\frac{2}{3} . In particular, our result includes the subcritical range − 1 < s ≤ − 2 3 -1<s\leq-\frac{2}{3} , which is above the scaling critical regularity s = − 1 s=-1 with respect to the Hölder-Besov scale. In view of the well-posedness result in 𝒞 s \mathcal{C}^{s} , s > − 2 3 s>-\frac{2}{3} , our ill-posedness result is sharp.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35K05

1.
Introduction

We consider
the cubic nonlinear heat equation (NLH):

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\Delta u+u^{3}=0\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}\ (x,t)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},

where
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{d}
.
The equation (
1.1
) and its variants (see, for example, (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) below) appear
in various physical settings and have been studied
extensively
from both theoretical and applied points of view.
See
[
37
,
15
,
5
,
33
]
for the classical well-posedness
results on (
1.1
).
Over the last two decades, there has been an increasing interest in
studying the equation (
1.1
) with
(singular) stochastic forcing and / or random initial data; see for example
[
13
,
19
,
17
,
18
]
.
Such study often requires the understanding of solutions to (
1.1
)
in the low regularity setting,
where the equation (
1.1
) is believed to be ill-posed.
However, the aforementioned ill-posedness seems to be part of the folklore,
and our main goal in this paper is to provide
a rigorous proof of this ill-posedness by exhibiting
a strong instability known as a
norm inflation
,
thus completing the well-posedness study of (
1.1
).

In the following, we study the equation (
1.1
) with respect to the Hölder-Besov space

\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})=B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, where the latter space
B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathcal{M})

denotes the usual Besov space defined by the norm:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,\infty}}=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}2^{js}\|\mathbf{P}_{j}u\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Here,
\mathbf{P}_{j}
denotes the non-homogeneous Littlewood-Paley projector
onto the frequencies
\{\xi\in\widehat{\mathcal{M}}:|\xi|\sim 2^{j}\}
for
j\geq 1

and
\{\xi\in\widehat{\mathcal{M}}:|\xi|\lesssim 1\}
for
j=0
, where

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
denotes the Pontryagin dual of
\mathcal{M}
,
i.e.
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
if
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
if
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
Recall the following Schauder estimate:

(1.3)

\displaystyle\|e^{t\Delta}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}(\mathcal{M})}\lesssim t^{-\frac{\sigma-s}{4}}\|e^{t\Delta}f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})}

for any
\sigma\geq s
and
0<t\leq 1
.
1
1

1

The upper bound on
t
is needed on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

When
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, (
1.3
) follows from
Young’s inequality and estimating
the
W^{{\sigma-s},1}
-norm of the heat kernel for
e^{t\Delta}
;
see
[
1
, Lemma 2.4]
(for the case
\sigma=s
).
When
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
(
1.3
)
follows from Young’s inequality
and the Poisson summation formula
to pass an estimate on (fractional derivatives of) the heat kernel on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
to that in a weighted Lebesgue space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; see the proof of
Lemma 2.3 in
[
23
]

for such a computation (but done for
e^{-t\sqrt{-\Delta}}
).
Then,
using the Schauder estimate (
1.3
),
one can easily show that (
1.1
) is locally well-posed
in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
for
s>-\frac{2}{3}
.
The well-posedness issue of (
1.1
) for
s\leq-\frac{2}{3}
, however,
appears unresolved.

As is well known, symmetries provide important heuristics
in studying well-posedness issues of nonlinear PDEs.
We now recall the scaling symmetry for (
1.1
);
if
u(x,t)
is a solution to (
1.1
)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

u^{\lambda}(x,t):=\lambda^{-1}u(\lambda^{-1}x,\lambda^{-2}t)

is also a solution to (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the scaled initial data
u^{\lambda}_{0}:=\lambda^{-1}u(\lambda^{-1}x)
.
A direct computation shows that
this scaling symmetry leaves the homogeneous Besov norm
\|u_{0}^{\lambda}\|_{\dot{B}^{-1}_{\infty,\infty}}

invariant, thus inducing
the scaling critical regularity
s_{\text{crit}}:=-1

with respect to the Hölder-Besov scale.
In studying an evolution equation with initial data
in a Banach space
B^{s}
of regularity
s
(think of
B^{s}
as
\mathcal{C}^{s}
, the Sobolev space
H^{s}
, etc.),
it is commonly conjectured that the equation is
well-posed in
B^{s}
for
s>s_{\text{crit}}
,
while it is ill-posed for
s<s_{\text{crit}}
.
In fact, on function spaces of positive regularities,
this conjecture has been confirmed affirmatively for many parabolic and dispersive PDEs.
However, working on function spaces of negative regularities,
this heuristics provided by the scaling argument is known to break down
in some instances; see
[
20
,
26
,
32
,
10
,
21
,
14
]

for ill-posedness results above critical regularities.
See also
[
3
,
8
,
20
,
29
,
31
,
30
]
for ill-posedness at critical regularities.

In this paper, we establish a norm inflation with infinite
loss of regularity for (
1.1
)
above
the scaling critical regularity
s_{\text{crit}}=-1
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Given
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
Let
s\leq-\frac{2}{3}
.
Then,
given any
\varepsilon>0
,
there exist a solution
u_{\varepsilon}
to (
1.1
) on
\mathcal{M}

and
t_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\varepsilon)
such that

(1.4)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})}<\varepsilon\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\|\mathbf{P}_{0}u_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})}>\varepsilon^{-1}.

Hölder–Besov spaces play a dominant role in parabolic singular SPDEs. The threshold
s>-\frac{2}{3}
appears in the study of the parabolic
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model
[
7
,
17
]
(see also
[
18
]
), and our results prove
that this threshold is optimal.

We also remark that, since
\|u_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}}\geq\|\mathbf{P}_{0}u_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}}
, the second estimate in (
1.4
)
implies
\|u_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}}>\varepsilon^{-1}
for any
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
.
When
\sigma=s
, the statement corresponds to the usual norm inflation
introduced in
[
12
]
in the context of
the nonlinear Schrödinger equations (NLS)
and the nonlinear wave equations.
In proving Theorem
1.1
,
we exploit a robust high-to-low energy transfer,
which allows us to prove (
1.4
), thus
leading to the so-called
“infinite loss of regularity”;
see, for example,
[
6
,
14
]
.
2
2

2

The work
[
14
]
does not state
their result as infinite loss of regularity but it follows from the proof.

Note that
Theorem
1.1
in particular implies discontinuity
of the solution
map
\Phi:u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})\mapsto u\in C([0,T];\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M}))

at the trivial function
u_{0}\equiv 0

for
s\leq-\frac{2}{3}
, thus establishing
ill-posedness of the cubic NLH (
1.1
).
Furthermore, in view of the aforementioned local well-posedness in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
for
s>-\frac{2}{3}
,
Theorem
1.1
is sharp.
See also Remark
1.5
(iii).

Our proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on the Fourier analytic approach

[
20
,
21
,
29
]
.
In
[
20
]
, Iwabuchi and Ogawa
introduced a new method for
proving ill-posedness of
evolution equations, exploiting high-to-low energy transfer
in the second Picard iterate.
In
[
21
]
, Kishimoto systematically
applied this approach
to prove ill-posedness of NLS.
See the work by Bejenaru and Tao
[
2
]

for a precursor of this approach.
We also mention the work by Bourgain and Pavlovíc
[
3
]

on the Navier-Stokes equations, where an analogous high-to-low energy transfer was exploited.
See also
[
38
,
35
]
.
In
[
20
,
21
]
,
the (scaled) modulation space
M_{2,1}

and its algebra property played an important role.
In
[
29
]
, the second author
implemented a slight simplification of this approach
by working on the Wiener algebra (instead of the modulation spaces).
We point out that the Hölder-Besov space
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
is
not a Fourier lattice
3
3

3

A Fourier lattice is a space,
where a norm depends only on the absolute value of the Fourier transform.

and thus is not quite suitable for this Fourier analytic approach
[
20
,
21
,
29
]
.
For example, in
[
20
,
26
]
, this method was applied
to prove ill-posedness of the (fractional) heat equations but the results were restricted to the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces
H^{s}

and the
L^{2}
-based Besov spaces
B^{s}_{2,q}
, both of which are Fourier lattices.
As we see below, our choice of initial data turns out to be rather simple (see (
3.1
) and (
4.1
) below),
enabling us to adapt this Fourier analytic approach to the
L^{\infty}
-based function spaces similar in spirit to
[
3
]
.

As in
[
20
,
21
,
29
]
,
we first express a solution
u
to (
1.1
) with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
in the power series expansion:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\Xi_{j}(u_{0}),

where
\Xi_{j}(u_{0})
denotes
homogeneous multilinear terms (in
u_{0}
) of
degree
2j+1
. In
[
20
,
21
]
,

\Xi_{j}
’s were defined by a recursive relation.
In proving Theorem
1.1
,
we instead follow the approach in
[
29
]

and define the
j
th term
\Xi_{j}
directly
via
the power series expansion indexed by trees.
See Section
2
.
There are several advantages to this latter approach.
First, it allows us to establish nonlinear estimates without an induction.
By a slight modification,
this approach allows us
to treat the Allen-Cahn equation (see (
1.7
) below)
in a straightforward manner.
Furthermore, arguing as in
[
29
]
, we can easily extend
Theorem
1.1

to a
norm inflation based at a general initial condition
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
;
given any
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
and any
\varepsilon>0
,
there exist a solution
u_{\varepsilon}
to (
1.1
)
and
t_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\varepsilon)
such that

(1.5)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon}(0)-u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})}<\varepsilon\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\|\mathbf{P}_{0}u_{\varepsilon}(t_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})}>\varepsilon^{-1},

thus establishing discontinuity of the solution map
everywhere in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.

We conclude this introduction by several remarks.

Remark 1.2
.

(i)
Unlike the dispersive case treated in
[
20
,
21
,
29
,
10
]
,
we work in the
L^{\infty}
-based function spaces
and thus our argument is insensitive to
dimensions
d\geq 1
.

(ii)
For the standard cubic nonlinearity, Choffrut and Pocovnicu
[
10
]

proved a norm inflation
for the fractional nonlinear Schrödinger equation:

i\partial_{t}u+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u+|u|^{2}u=0

above the scaling critical regularity, provided that
\alpha>2
.
Namely, a dispersion stronger than the standard Laplacian was needed.
It is interesting to compare this with the parabolic case (Theorem
1.1
),
where a norm inflation above the scaling critical regularity
hold true for the standard Laplacian.
See also
[
21
]
for a norm inflation above the scaling critical regularity
for the quadratic NLS with the less physical nonlinearity
|u|^{2}
.

Remark 1.3
.

(i) While we present the result for the defocusing case (i.e. with the
+
-sign in (
1.1
)),
our argument is insensitive to the defocusing / focusing nature of the nonlinearity and thus it also applies to the focusing case:

(1.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u-\Delta u-u^{3}=0.

Furthermore, our argument is robust so that it can be applied to treat
a general power-type nonlinearity
u^{k}
.
See
[
21
,
14
]
in the case of the nonlinear Schrödinger equations
and the nonlinear wave equations with
a general power-type nonlinearity.

(ii) As mentioned above, a slight modification of the proof of Theorem

1.1

yields a norm inflation with a general initial data as stated in (
1.5
).
See
[
29
]
for such an argument.

Remark 1.4
.

Consider
the
Allen-Cahn equation:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u-\Delta u-u+u^{3}=0.

By the Schauder estimate, one can easily prove local well-posedness of (
1.7
)
in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
for
s>-\frac{2}{3}
.
On the other hand, a slight modification of the proof of Theorem
1.1

yields a norm inflation for (
1.7
) in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
for
s\leq-\frac{2}{3}
.
See Remark
3.1
.

Next, consider
the Cahn-Hilliard equation:

(1.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+\Delta^{2}u-\Delta u^{3}=0.

and its variants:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+\Delta^{2}u-u^{2}\Delta u=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad\partial_{t}u+\Delta^{2}u-u(\partial u)(\partial u)=0.

These equations all scale the same way and the scaling critical regularity is
s=-1

in the Hölder-Besov scale.
By the Schauder estimate:

(1.10)

\displaystyle\|e^{-t\Delta^{2}}u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{\sigma-s}{4}}\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}}

for any
\sigma\geq s
and
0<t\leq 1
,
4
4

4

As in (
1.3
), the upper bound on
t
is needed on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

we can prove local well-posedness of the Cahn-Hilliard equation (
1.8
)
in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
for the full subcritical range
s>-1
.
On the other hand, as for the equations in (
1.9
),
the Schauder estimate (
1.10
) yields local well-posedness
in
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\mathcal{M})
only for a partial range
s>-\frac{2}{3}
.
In fact, for these variant equations in (
1.9
),
our argument works and yields
a norm inflation as in Theorem
1.1
holds for
s\leq-\frac{2}{3}
.
See Remark
3.1
.

Due to the presence of the Laplacian on the cubic nonlinearity in (
1.8
),
our strategy for proving norm inflation via the high-to-low energy transfer
(or using high-to-high energy transfer) does not work for
the Cahn-Hilliard equation (
1.8
).
It is of interest to note
how the equations in (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) scale the same way
but that their well / ill-posedness results are quite different.
We point out that, as for proving a norm inflation,
it seems easier to treat a nonlinearity that is not a total derivative,
as in (
1.9
).
See also
[
9
,
36
]
.

Remark 1.5
.

(i) It is worthwhile to note that the threshold regularity
s=-\frac{2}{3}
also appears in the study
of long-time behavior of small data solutions.
In
[
25
]
, Miao, Yuan, and Zhang
proved small data global well-posedness of (
1.1
)
and (
1.6
) with small initial data in
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
where
s=\frac{d}{p}-1
,
d<p<3d
, and
1\leq q\leq\infty
.
Here, the regularity
s=\frac{d}{p}-1
corresponds to the scaling critical regularity
for the
L^{p}
-based Besov spaces.
Note that the condition on
p
implies that
-\frac{2}{3}<s<0
.
In
[
4
]
,
Brandolese and Cortez proved
existence of a (smooth) finite time blowup solution to (
1.6
)
with small initial data in
\dot{B}^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{3d,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
3<q\leq\infty
,
thus providing a negative answer to Meyer’s question
[
24
]
.

(ii) By a slight modification of the proof,
we can extend the norm inflation to the
L^{p}
-based Besov spaces
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

for any
1\leq p\leq\infty

in the case of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}

for (a)
s<-\frac{2}{3}
and
1\leq q\leq\infty

and (b)
s=-\frac{2}{3}
and
3<q\leq\infty
.
5
5

5

In the endpoint case
s=-\frac{2}{3}
,
we need to slightly modify the proof by replacing

\log K
in (
4.1
) by
K^{\frac{1}{3}-\delta}
. The restriction on
q

is needed to have the
B^{s}_{p,q}
-norm of the initial data tend to 0 as in (
4.3
).

On
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we need to slightly modify our choice of initial data by localizing in space,
but we expect the same result also holds on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(iii)
Recall the following characterization of the Besov norm (
[
22
, Theorem 5.3]
):

(1.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|f\|_{B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}}&\sim\|e^{\Delta}f\|_{L^{\infty}}+\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}t^{-1}\|t^{\frac{1}{3}}e^{t\Delta}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}^{3}dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}}\\
&=\|e^{\Delta}f\|_{L^{\infty}}+\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\|e^{t\Delta}f\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3}dt\bigg)^{\frac{1}{3}}.\end{split}

Using this characterization,
it is straightforward to prove local well-posedness
of (
1.1
) in
B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
For example, one can first run a contraction argument
in
L^{3}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
to construct a solution
u\in L^{3}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))

to (
1.1
),
and then a posteriori show that
u
belongs to
C([0,T];B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
.
6
6

6

Here,
as in
[
27
,
28
]
,
we view the Besov space
B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

as the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
under the norm (
1.11
).
Alternatively, i.e. if we view the Besov space
B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as the collection
of elements in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with finite
B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}
-norms,
then a solution
u
to (
1.1
) does not belong to

C([0,T];B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
in general.
With this latter definition of the Besov space, even the linear solution
e^{t\Delta}u_{0}

does not belong to

C([0,T];B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
in general.
In this case, we can actually show that, given

u_{0}\in B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
the nonlinear part

v(t):=u(t)-e^{t\Delta}u_{0}
belongs to

C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and
that the map
u_{0}\mapsto v=v(u_{0})
is locally Lipschitz with respect to
u_{0}\in B^{-\frac{2}{3}}_{\infty,3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Therefore,
our results (Theorem
1.1
and Remark
1.5
(ii) above) are sharp across the entire Besov scale
B^{s}_{\infty,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
.

Remark 1.6
.

Using probabilistic methods, the first author
[
9
]
recently showed the same type of norm inflation as (
1.4
) in the scaling subcritical space
\mathcal{C}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

(but not in
B^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{\infty,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for finite
q<\infty
)
for a heat equation with dominant nonlinearity
u\times Du
that is not a total derivative,
where the scaling critical space is
\mathcal{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

2.
Preliminary analysis

In this section, we review a basic local well-posedness result for (
1.1
)
and the power series expansion of a solution. While the following presentation closely follows
that in
[
29
]
, we include details for readers’ convenience.

We say that
u
is a solution to (
1.1
)
with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

if
u
satisfies the following Duhamel formulation:

(2.1)

\displaystyle u(t)=e^{t\Delta}u_{0}+\mathcal{I}[u](t),

where
\mathcal{I}
denotes the
the Duhamel integral operator
defined by

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}[u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}](t):=-\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-t^{\prime})\Delta}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{3}u_{j}(t^{\prime})\bigg)dt^{\prime}

with a shorthand notation
\mathcal{I}[u]:=\mathcal{I}[u,u,u]

when all the three arguments
u_{1},u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
are identical.
Then, from the boundedness of the heat semigroup
and the algebra property of

\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
,
s_{0}>0
, we have the following local well-posedness of (
1.1
)
in
\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
,
s_{0}>0
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
s_{0}>0
. Then,
the cubic NLH (
1.1
) is locally well-posed in
\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
.
More precisely, given
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
,
there exist
T\sim\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}}^{-2}>0
and
a unique solution
u\in C([0,T];\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))

satisfying (
2.1
).

Fix
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
for some
s_{0}>0
,
and let
u
be the solution to (
1.1
) with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

constructed in Lemma
2.1
.
Following the approach in
[
29
]
, we express the solution
u
in the power series expansion;
see
[
34
,
16
,
11
]
, where the power series expansion was used to construct solutions
to parabolic and dispersive equations.
We first recall the following definition of (ternary) trees.

Definition 2.2
.

(i) Given a partially ordered set
\mathcal{T}
with partial order
\leq
,
we say that
b\in\mathcal{T}

with
b\leq a
and
b\neq a

is a child of
a\in\mathcal{T}
,
if
b\leq c\leq a
implies
either
c=a
or
c=b
.
If the latter condition holds, we also say that
a
is the parent of
b
.

(ii)
A tree
\mathcal{T}
is a finite partially ordered set, satisfying
the following properties:

(a)

Let
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathcal{T}
.
If
a_{4}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{1}
and

a_{4}\leq a_{3}\leq a_{1}
, then we have
a_{2}\leq a_{3}
or
a_{3}\leq a_{2}
.

(b)

A node
a\in\mathcal{T}
is called terminal, if it has no child.
A non-terminal node
a\in\mathcal{T}
is a node
with exactly three children.

(c)

There exists a maximal element
r\in\mathcal{T}
(called the root node) such that
a\leq r
for all
a\in\mathcal{T}
.

(d)

\mathcal{T}
consists of the disjoint union of
\mathcal{T}^{0}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\infty}
,
where
\mathcal{T}^{0}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\infty}

denote the collections of non-terminal nodes and terminal nodes, respectively.

Note that the number
|\mathcal{T}|
of nodes in a tree
\mathcal{T}
is
3j+1
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
where
|\mathcal{T}^{0}|=j
and
|\mathcal{T}^{\infty}|=2j+1
.
Let us denote the collection of trees in the
j
th generation (i.e. with
j
parental nodes) by
{\bf T}(j)
, i.e.

{\bf T}(j):=\big\{\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{T}\text{ is a tree with }|\mathcal{T}|=3j+1\big\}.

Then, there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\#{\bf T}(j)\leq C_{0}^{j}

for any
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
See
[
29
]
for the proof of (
2.3
).

We now define a map
\Psi=\Psi_{u_{0}}:\bigcup_{j=0}^{\infty}{\bf T}(j)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,T])

as follows.
Given a tree
\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}(j)
,

j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
we define
\Psi(\mathcal{T})
by the following rules:

(i)

Replace a non-terminal node “
”
by the Duhamel integral operator
\mathcal{I}
defined in (
2.2
)
with its three children as arguments
u_{1},u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
.

(ii)

Replace a terminal node “
”
by the linear solution
e^{t\Delta}u_{0}
.

Note that, if
\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}(j)
,
then
\Psi(\mathcal{T})
is
(2j+1)
-linear in
u_{0}
.
For example, we have

\Psi

to formal sums of trees via linearity
and applying
\Psi
to (
2.6

1.1

u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 16.93 0 C 16.93 0.87 16.22 1.57 15.35 1.57 C 14.48 1.57 13.78 0.87 13.78 0 C 13.78 -0.87 14.48 -1.57 15.35 -1.57 C 16.22 -1.57 16.93 -0.87 16.93 0 Z M 15.35 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\big)+\cdots.\end{split}

Then, from
(
2.7
) with
(
2.2
),
(
2.3
), (
2.4
),
the boundedness of the heat semigroup,
and the algebra property of

\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
,
s_{0}>0
,
we have the following convergence result.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
s_{0}>0
. Then, given
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
,
the power series expansion in (
2.7
) converges
absolutely in
C([0,T];\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))
,
provided that
T\leq c_{0}\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}}^{-2}
for some small
c_{0}>0
.

It is easy to check that
u
defined by the power series in (
2.7
)
indeed satisfies the Duhamel formulation (
2.1
).
Note that the time
T\sim\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}}^{-2}>0
for the local well-posedness in Lemma
2.1

and for the convergence of the power series in Lemma
2.3

can be chosen to be the same.
Thanks to the unconditional uniqueness of the solution in
the class
\mathcal{C}([0,T];\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M}))
,
we conclude that

u
defined by the power series in (
2.7
) coincides with
the solution constructed in Lemma
2.1
.

3.
Non-endpoint case:
s<-\frac{2}{3}

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
1.1
for
s<-\frac{2}{3}
.
We will treat the endpoint case
s=-\frac{2}{3}
in the next section.
Given
s<-\frac{2}{3}
, the
\mathcal{C}^{s}
-norm is controlled by
the
\mathcal{C}^{-\frac{2}{3}}
-norm and thus
Theorem
1.1
for the non-endpoint case
s<-\frac{2}{3}

follows from that for the endpoint case

s=-\frac{2}{3}
whose proof is presented in the next section.
For readers’ convenience, however,
we decided to include
the proof of Theorem
1.1
for the non-endpoint case, since the relevant argument is much simpler than that for the endpoint case
and thus is easier to follow.
As in the previous work
[
20
,
21
,
29
]
, the main strategy is to choose a suitable initial condition
u_{0}=u_{0}(N)
,
depending on a large parameter
N\gg 1
and vanishing as
N\to\infty
,
and show that the Picard second iterate
\Xi_{1}(u_{0})
diverges as
N\to\infty
,
while keeping the other terms under control.
In order to achieve the growth of the Picard second iterate,
we exploit the high
\times
high
\times
high-to-low energy transfer mechanism
of the cubic nonlinearity.

Note that in proving Theorem
1.1
, the
\mathcal{C}^{s}
-norm appears
only
in the first estimate of (
1.4
), where we need to establish an upper bound.
Thus, in view of
\|u\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}}
for
\sigma\leq s
,
it suffices to only consider the subcritical case
-1<s<-\frac{2}{3}
.
Fix a large integer
N\gg 1
.
Then, we set the initial condition
u_{0}=u_{0}(N)
as

(3.1)

\displaystyle u_{0}(x)=N^{-s-\varepsilon}\big(\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)+\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big)

for some small
\varepsilon>0
,
where
n_{1}=(N,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Then, from (
1.2
), we have

\displaystyle\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}}\sim N^{-\varepsilon}\longrightarrow 0,

as
N\to\infty
. This shows the first claim in (
1.4
).

From (
3.1
), we can write the linear solution as

(3.2)

\begin{split}e^{t\Delta}u_{0}(x)&=N^{-s-\varepsilon}\big(e^{-t|n_{1}|^{2}}\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)+e^{-t4|n_{1}|^{2}}\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big)\\
&=N^{-s-\varepsilon}\big(e^{-tN^{2}}\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)+e^{-4tN^{2}}\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big),\end{split}

and thus we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}e^{t\Delta}u_{0}=0.

By a direct computation, we have

\displaystyle(e^{t\Delta}u_{0}(x))^{3}

\displaystyle=N^{-3s-3\varepsilon}\big(e^{-3tN^{2}}\cos^{3}(n_{1}\cdot x)+3e^{-6tN^{2}}\cos^{2}(n_{1}\cdot x)\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)

\displaystyle\quad+3e^{-9tN^{2}}\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)\cos^{2}(2n_{1}\cdot x)+e^{-12tN^{2}}\cos^{3}(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big).

Using the trigonometric identities:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\cos^{3}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\cos 3x+3\cos x),

\displaystyle\cos(x_{1})\cos(x_{2})\cos(x_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3}\in\{1,-1\}}\cos(x_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}x_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}x_{3}),

we then have

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(e^{t\Delta}u_{0}(x))^{3}&=\frac{1}{4}N^{-3s-3\varepsilon}\Big[e^{-3tN^{2}}(\cos(3n_{1}\cdot x)+3\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)\big)\\
&\hphantom{XX}+3e^{-6tN^{2}}\big(1+\cos(4n_{1}\cdot x)+2\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big)\\
&\hphantom{XX}+3e^{-9tN^{2}}\big(\cos(5n_{1}\cdot x)+2\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)+\cos(3n_{1}\cdot x)\big)\\
&\hphantom{XX}+e^{-12tN^{2}}\big(\cos(6n_{1}\cdot x)+3\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big)\Big].\end{split}

Hence, from (
2.4
) and (
3.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Xi_{1}(u_{0})(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-t^{\prime})\Delta}(e^{t^{\prime}\Delta}u_{0})^{3}dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}N^{-2-3s-3\varepsilon}(1-e^{-6tN^{2}})+\frac{1}{4}N^{-3s-3\varepsilon}\Big[e^{-9tN^{2}}\frac{e^{6tN^{2}}-1}{6N^{2}}\cos\big(3n_{1}\cdot x)

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+3e^{-tN^{2}}\frac{1-e^{-2tN^{2}}}{2N^{2}}\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)\big)+3e^{-16tN^{2}}\frac{e^{10tN^{2}}-1}{10N^{2}}\cos(4n_{1}\cdot x)

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+3e^{-4tN^{2}}\frac{1-e^{-2tN^{2}}}{N^{2}}\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)+3e^{-25tN^{2}}\frac{e^{16tN^{2}}-1}{16N^{2}}\cos(5n_{1}\cdot x)

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+3e^{-tN^{2}}\frac{1-e^{-8tN^{2}}}{4N^{2}}\cos(n_{1}\cdot x)+3te^{-9tN^{2}}\cos(3n_{1}\cdot x)

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+e^{-36tN^{2}}\frac{e^{24tN^{2}}-1}{24N^{2}}\cos(6n_{1}\cdot x)+3e^{-4tN^{2}}\frac{1-e^{-8tN^{2}}}{8N^{2}}\cos(2n_{1}\cdot x)\big)\Big].

In particular, for
N\gg 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{1}(u_{0})(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}N^{-2-3s-3\varepsilon}(1-e^{-6tN^{2}}),

where
\mathbf{P}_{0}
denotes the Littlewood-Paley frequency projector
onto the frequencies

\{\xi\in\widehat{\mathcal{M}}:|\xi|\lesssim 1\}
.
Therefore, by choosing
t=N^{-2+2\varepsilon}
such that
e^{-6tN^{2}}\ll 1
for
N\gg 1
,
we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{1}(u_{0})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\sim N^{-2-3s-3\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\infty,

as
N\to\infty
, provided that
s<-\frac{2}{3}-\varepsilon
.
Given
s<-\frac{2}{3}
,
this last condition can be guaranteed by choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Next, we estimate the contribution from the higher order terms in (
2.7
).
From
(
2.4
), (
2.3
),
the boundedness of the heat semigroup on
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
,
and the algebra property of
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
together with (
3.1
)
and
t=N^{-2+2\varepsilon}
,
we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg\|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{j}(u_{0})(t)\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}&\lesssim\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\|\Xi_{j}(u_{0})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
&\leq\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}C_{0}^{j}t^{j}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2j+1}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}C_{0}^{j}N^{(-2-2s)j-s-\varepsilon}\\
&\lesssim N^{-4-5s-\varepsilon}\end{split}

for
N\gg 1
,
provided that
-2-2s<0
, namely

s>-1
.
Here, we used that, by Lemmas
2.1
and
2.3
with (
3.1
), it suffices to have

t=N^{-2+2\varepsilon}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}}^{-2}\sim N^{2s+2\varepsilon-2s_{0}}
for some
s_{0}>0
to guarantee local well-posedness
and convergence of the power series expansion (
2.7
).
8
8

8

Here, we need

s_{0}>0
such that
\mathcal{C}^{s_{0}}(\mathcal{M})
is an algebra, which is crucial for Lemmas
2.1
and
2.3
.

This condition reduces to

s\geq-1+s_{0}
, which can be guaranteed
for given
s>-1
by choosing
s_{0}>0
sufficiently small.
We point out that
the right-hand side of (
3.7
) is controlled by (
3.6
), provided that

s>-1+\varepsilon
.
Given
s>-1
,
this last condition can be guaranteed by choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Finally, putting
(
2.7
),
(
3.3
),
(
3.6
),
and (
3.7
) together, we have

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{P}_{0}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&\geq\|\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{1}(u_{0})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}-\|\mathbf{P}_{0}e^{t\Delta}u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}-\bigg\|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{j}(t)\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
&\gtrsim N^{-2-3s-3\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\infty,\end{split}

as
N\to\infty
, provided that

(3.9)

\displaystyle-1+\varepsilon<s<-\frac{2}{3}-\varepsilon

and
t=N^{-2+2\varepsilon}
.
This proves Theorem
1.1
for
s<-\frac{2}{3}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In this remark, we indicate how to modify the argument to treat the
Allen-Cahn equation (
1.7
) and the
Cahn-Hilliard-type equation (
1.9
).
We only discuss the case
-1<s<-\frac{2}{3}
.

We first consider the Allen-Cahn equation (
1.7
).
Note that the difference
between the heat semigroup and the linear semigroup

e^{t(1+\Delta)}
for the Allen-Cahn equation appears only in the low frequencies.
Moreover, in proving a norm inflation, we study the dynamics only for very short times
and thus their difference becomes negligible. Hence, the argument presented above
can be applied directly to (
1.7
).
In the following, however, we present an alternative argument,
where we view
u-u^{3}
as a combined power-type nonlinearity
and modify the structure of trees to allow nodes with different numbers of children.
We decided to include this argument since it can be adapted to
study the nonlinear heat equation with a combined power-type nonlinearity
(such as the cubic-quintic nonlinearity).
See also
[
36
]
, where ternary-quinary trees
were used to prove a norm inflation for the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation.

By writing (
1.7
) in the Duhamel formulation, we have

\displaystyle u(t)=e^{t\Delta}+\mathcal{I}^{1}[u]+\mathcal{I}[u],

where
\mathcal{I}
is as in (
2.2
)
and

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{1}[u]:=-\mathcal{I}[u,1,1].

Thus, we need to modify Definition
2.2
to take
this extra term
\mathcal{I}^{1}[u]
into account.
More precisely, we need to replace the condition (b) by

(b’)

A node
a\in\mathcal{T}
is called terminal, if it has no child.
A non-terminal node
a\in\mathcal{T}
is a node
with exactly one child or three children.

Given a tree
\mathcal{T}
with this new definition,
let
|\mathcal{T}|_{1}
(and
|\mathcal{T}|_{3}
, respectively) denotes the numbers of nodes
which have one child (and three children, respectively).
Then, for
j_{1},j_{3}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
we set

\displaystyle{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle:=\big\{\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{T}\text{ is a tree with }(|\mathcal{T}|_{1},|\mathcal{T}|_{3})=(j_{1},j_{3})\}.

Note that for
\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{3})
,
we have

|\mathcal{T}|=j_{1}+3j_{3}+1
.
Furthermore, arguing as in the proof of Lemma 2.3 in
[
29
]
,
the following bound on the size of
{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{3})
holds;
there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=j_{1}+j_{3}}\#{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{3})\leq C_{0}^{j}

for any
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

We define
a map

\Psi=\Psi_{u_{0}}:\bigcup_{j_{1},j_{3}=0}^{\infty}{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{3})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}\times[0,T])

by replacing the definition (i) by

(i’)

Replace a non-terminal node “
” with three children
by the Duhamel integral operator
\mathcal{I}
defined in (
2.2
)
with its three children as arguments
u_{1},u_{2}
, and
u_{3}
,
and replace a non-terminal node “
” with one child
by
\mathcal{I}^{1}
defined in (
3.10
) with its child as an argument
u
.

We then set

\displaystyle\Xi_{j}(u_{0}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{3})\\
j=j_{1}+j_{3}\end{subarray}}\Psi(\mathcal{T}).

Then, the power series expansion for a (smooth) solution
u
to (
1.7
)
is given by

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\Xi_{j}(u_{0})=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}^{1,3}(j_{1},j_{3})\\
j=j_{1}+j_{3}\end{subarray}}\Psi(\mathcal{T}).

We point out that Lemmas
2.1
and
2.3
also hold for (
1.7
).
Then, with the bound (
2.3
),
we can simply repeat the argument presented above
to conclude a norm inflation for (
1.7
).

Next, we consider the Cahn-Hilliard-type equation (
1.9
).
Let us consider the first equation. A similar argument applies to the second
equation in (
1.9
).
In this case, we need to use the following Duhamel integral operator:

(3.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}[u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}](t):=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\Delta^{2}}u_{1}(t^{\prime})u_{2}(t^{\prime})\Delta u_{3}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

With the same choice (
3.1
) for the initial data,
by repeating the computation above,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}(u_{0})(t)

\displaystyle=c_{0}N^{-2-3s-3\varepsilon}(1-e^{-6tN^{4}}),

where
\widetilde{\Xi}_{j}
is defined as in (
2.4
) but with the Duhamel
integral operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
in defined (
3.11
).
Hence, by choosing
t=N^{-4+2\varepsilon}
,
the growth (
3.6
) still holds for
s<-\frac{2}{3}-\varepsilon
.

On the other hand,
we claim that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\Xi}_{j}(u_{0})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{0}^{j}t^{\frac{j}{2}}\|u_{0}\|^{2j+1}_{L^{\infty}}.

For now, assume (
3.12
) and we bound the higher order terms.
With the choice of
t
as above, (
3.12
), and (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}_{0}\widetilde{\Xi}_{j}(u_{0})(t)\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}C_{0}^{j}t^{\frac{j}{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2j+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}C_{0}^{j}N^{(-4-2s)j-s-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-4-5s-\varepsilon}

just as in (
3.7
),
provided that
-4-2s<0
, namely

s>-2
.
Hence, a norm inflation holds for
the first equation in (
1.9
).

It now remains to prove (
3.12
).
With (
2.4
) (for
\widetilde{\Xi}_{j}(u_{0})
) and (
2.3
),
it suffices to prove

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|\Psi(\mathcal{T})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{j}{2}}\|u_{0}\|^{2j+1}_{L^{\infty}}

for each
\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}(j)
. We prove (
3.13
) by induction.
When
j=1
,
the bound (
3.13
) follows from
the Schauder estimate (
1.10
) (but for
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
):

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\Delta e^{-t\Delta^{2}}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Now, fix
j_{0}\geq 2
and assume that (
3.13
) holds for any
j\leq j_{0}-1
.
Given
\mathcal{T}\in{\bf T}(j_{0})
,
suppose that there exists a terminal node
a\in\mathcal{T}^{\infty}

whose parent
b
is not the third child of its parent.
9
9

9

Here, the third child
corresponds to
u_{3}
in (
3.11
) at the level of the Duhamel integral operator.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{a}
denote the sub-tree in
\mathcal{T}
of one generation, containing
a\in\mathcal{T}^{\infty}

as one of its terminal nodes and
b
as the parental node.
Then, let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
be the tree obtained from
\mathcal{T}
by replacing the sub-tree
\mathcal{T}_{a}

with a node
b

(and thus
b
becomes a terminal node).
Note that
\mathcal{T}
can be constructed by replacing
the terminal node
b\in(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})^{\infty}
by the tree
\mathcal{T}_{a}
.
By applying the inductive hypothesis
twice, we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi(\mathcal{T})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{j_{0}-1}{2}}\|u_{0}\|^{2j_{0}-2}_{L^{\infty}}\|\Psi(\mathcal{T}_{a})\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\frac{j_{0}}{2}}\|u_{0}\|^{2j_{0}+1}_{L^{\infty}},

yielding (
3.13
) for
j=j_{0}
in this case.
Hence, it remains to consider the case when there is no terminal node
whose parent is not the third child of its parent.
In other words, every parent in this (unbalanced) tree is the third child of its parent.
In this case, by the Schauder estimate (
3.14
), we have

(i)

for the last generation:
10
10

10

Here, we used the fact that

\int_{0}^{t}(t-t^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{2}}dt^{\prime}=B(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
,
where
B(a,b)
is the beta function.

\displaystyle\|\Delta\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}[e^{-t\Delta^{2}}u_{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3},

(ii)

for the intermediate generations:

\displaystyle\|\Delta\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}[e^{-t\Delta^{2}}u_{0},e^{-t\Delta^{2}}u_{0},u_{3}]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|u_{3}\|_{L^{\infty}},

(iii)

for the first generation:

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}[e^{-t\Delta^{2}}u_{0},e^{-t\Delta^{2}}u_{0},u_{3}]\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|u_{3}\|_{L^{\infty}},

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}](t):=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\Delta^{2}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{3}u_{j}(t^{\prime})\bigg)dt^{\prime}.

Putting (i), (ii), and (iii) together,
we directly obtain (
3.13
) in this case (without using the inductive hypothesis).
Therefore, by induction, (
3.13
) holds for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

4.
Endpoint case:
s=-\frac{2}{3}

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
1.1

at the endpoint regularity
s=-\frac{2}{3}
.
When
s=-\frac{2}{3}
, the construction in the previous section (with
\varepsilon=0
)
only gives the
O(1)
growth of the Picard second iterate.
In order to overcome this difficulty, we consider a lacunary sequence
of initial data of type (
3.1
), where each component yields
O(1)
contribution
to the Picard second iterate at the frequency 0.

Fix large integers
N,K\gg 1
.
Then, we set the initial condition
u_{0}=u_{0}(N,K)
as

(4.1)

\displaystyle u_{0}(x)=\frac{1}{\log K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(a_{k}N)^{\frac{2}{3}}\big(\cos(a_{k}n_{1}\cdot x)+\cos(2a_{k}n_{1}\cdot x)\big),

where
n_{1}=(N,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
as before and

(4.2)

\displaystyle a_{k}=2^{2^{k}}.

Then, from (
1.2
), we have

(4.3)

\displaystyle\|u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{-\frac{2}{3}}}\sim\frac{1}{\log K}\longrightarrow 0,

as
K\to\infty
. This shows the first claim in (
1.4
).

Proceeding as in (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) with (
4.1
), we have

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}e^{t\Delta}u_{0}(t)=0.

By repeating the computation in the previous section and making use
of the lacunarity of the summands in (
4.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{0}(e^{t\Delta}u_{0})^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4(\log K)^{3}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(a_{k}N)^{2}e^{-6t(a_{k}N)^{2}}.

Hence, by choosing
t=N^{-2+\delta}
for some small
\delta>0
, we have

(4.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{1}(u_{0})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&=\bigg\|\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-t^{\prime})\Delta}\mathbf{P}_{0}(e^{t^{\prime}\Delta}u_{0})^{3}dt^{\prime}\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
&=\frac{1}{8(\log K)^{3}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(1-e^{-6t(a_{k}N)^{2}})\\
&\gtrsim\frac{K}{(\log K)^{3}}\longrightarrow\infty,\end{split}

as
K\to\infty
.

It remains to estimate the contribution from the higher order terms in (
2.7
).
Proceeding as in the previous section with (
4.1
), (
4.2
),
and
t=N^{-2+\delta}
and choosing
K=c_{0}\log\log N
for some small
c_{0}>0
, we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg\|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}_{0}\Xi_{j}(u_{0})(t)\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}&\lesssim\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\|\Xi_{j}(u_{0})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}C_{0}^{j}t^{j}\|u_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2j+1}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{C_{0}^{j}K^{2j+1}}{(\log K)^{2j+1}}2^{(2j+1)2^{K}}N^{(-\frac{2}{3}+\delta)j+\frac{2}{3}}\\
&\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

as
N\to\infty
.

Finally, putting
(
4.3
), (
4.4
), (
4.5
), and (
4.6
) together
with
t=N^{-2+\delta}
and
K=c_{0}\log\log N
for some small
\delta,c_{0}>0

and proceeding as in (
3.8
),
we conclude the proof of Theorem
1.1

at the endpoint case
s=-\frac{2}{3}
.
